where the index 2j is assumed to be reduced modulo n. But the right-hand side is 1, because
the numerators and denominators both run through the same values. Thus c0 = ±1, and we are
done.

Lemma 14.7. Let n ≥ 3 be odd. Then λn is sPV if and only if there are no j ∈ Z∗n such that
n/3 ≤ j ≤ 2n/3, except for (n ± 1)/2.

Proof. Set k := (n − 1)/2. By the previous lemma, λn is an algebraic integer with conjugates
(2 + 2 cos(2jπ/n))−1 = (2 + ζ j + ζ −j )−1 for j ∈ Z∗n with j < n/2. We can drop the requirement
that j < n/2, because cos(2jπ/n) = cos(2(n − j)π/n) for all j. Since λn = (2 + ζ k + ζ −k )−1 =
(2 + ζ k+1 + ζ −(k+1) )−1 , the conjugates of λn other than λn itself are of the form (2 + ζ j + ζ −j )−1 =
(2 + 2 cos(2jπ/n))−1 for all j ∈ Z∗n \ {k, k + 1}. It follows by definition that λn is sPV if and only
if 0 < (2 + 2 cos(2jπ/n))−1 < 1, or equivalently, cos(2jπ/n) > −1/2 for all such j. This latter
inequality is equivalent to j < n/3 or j > 2n/3, for all j ∈ Z∗n other than k or k + 1.

Lemma 14.8. If n > 21 and n is odd, then there exists a j ∈ Z∗n such that n/3 < j < 2n/3 and
j∈
 / {(n − 1)/2, (n + 1)/2}.

Proof. We give an elementary proof using only the Bertrand-Chebyshev theorem, which states that
for all integers m > 1, there exists a prime p with m < p < 2m. It follows that this must also be
true for all real m > 1, by applying the theorem to bmc if m ≥ 2. Set k := (n − 1)/2 as usual.

Case 1: n = qp where p is an odd prime and q ∈ {5, 7, 9, 11}. Choose two numbers j1 < j2
    depending on p and q according to the following table:

                                                 q        j1          j2
                                                 5      2p − 1      2p + 1
                                                 7      3p − 1      3p + 1
                                                 9      4p − 2      4p − 1
                                                11      5p − 2      5p − 1

      Since n is sufficiently large, one can readily check that: (i) n/3 < j1 < j2 < k; (ii) neither
      j1 nor j2 is a multiple of p; and (iii) at least one of j1 and j2 is coprime with q and hence
      coprime with n, satisfying the lemma.

Case 2: not Case 1 and neither k nor k + 1 is prime. By Bertrand-Chebyshev, there exists a prime
    j such that n/3 < j < 2n/3. Then j - n and j is neither k nor k + 1, so j satisfies the lemma.

Case 3: not Case 1, one of k and k + 1 is prime, and the other is not of the form 2p for any prime
    p. By Bertrand-Chebyshev, there exists a prime r such that n/6 < r < n/3. We must have
    r - n, because we are not in Case 1 (and n is odd). Then setting j := 2r satisfies the lemma.


                                                         77
Case 4: not Case 1, one of k and k + 1 is prime, and the other is of the form 2p for some prime p.
    By Bertrand-Chebyshev, choose a prime s such that n/12 < s < n/6. Again, s - n since we
    are not in Case 1 (and n is odd). Then setting j := 4s satisfies the lemma.

Cases 1–4 are clearly exhaustive.

Proof of Proposition 14.5. Lemmas 14.7 and 14.8 imply that λn cannot be sPV if n > 21. For 3 ≤
n ≤ 21, one can check case by case that Lemma 14.7 is satisfied if and only if n ∈ {3, 5, 7, 9, 15}.

n = 5, 7, 9, 15 as in Proposition 14.5 and for selected m ≥ 3 dividing 2n (the n = 3 case is trivial,
as λ3 = 1). We order them by increasing n then increasing m for each n. Each displayed set has
dihedral Dm symmetry, but none has any translational symmetry. Each displayed set is a Meyer
set by Corollary 15.42.

                                                   91
14.4    Regular convex polyhedra
In this section, we investigate cases where λ-convex closures of the five regular convex polyhedra
(Platonic solids) in R3 are uniformly discrete, for real λ. We show that for any real sPV λ, the λ-
convex closure of the tetrahedron, cube, and octahedron are uniformly discrete. Berman & Moody
showed that the (1 + ϕ)-convex closures of the dodecahedron and icosahedron are both uniformly
discrete using icosians (a finite subgroup of the multiplicative group of the quaterions) [4]. We give
an independent, geometry-based proof. We prove all these results using the projection technique
we first used in the proof of Lemma 14.1. In fact, one can think of the next proposition as a
three-dimensional version of Lemma 14.1.

Proposition 14.10. For any sPV λ ∈ R, the λ-convex closures of the sets of corners of the regular
tetrahedron, cube, and octahedron are all uniformly discrete point sets in R3 .

Proof. We prove the result for the regular tetrahedron; the other two have similar proofs. For
concreteness—and consistency with how we handled the regular polygons above—we orient the
tetrahedron in R3 so that

   • one of its edges coincides with the set {(x, 0, 0) | 0 ≤ x ≤ 1},

   • one of the faces incident to this edge lies in the half-plane {(x, y, 0) | y ≥ 0}, and

   • the tetrahedron itself lies in the half-space {(x, y, z) | z ≥ 0}.

We let T ⊆ R3 be the four corners of this tetrahedron. The perpendicular projection of T into the
x, y-plane is                                      √           √
                      T 0 := {(0, 0), (1, 0), (1/2, 3/2), (1/2, 3/6)} ⊆ R2 ,
shown below, with edges included:
                                              y
                                                        √ 
                                                      1
                                                      2
                                                        , 23

                                                                       √ 
                                                                     1
                                                                     2
                                                                       , 63




                                                                        x
                                     (0, 0)                    (1, 0)

Projecting T 0 perpendicularly onto the x-axis yields the set T 00 = {0, 12 , 1}. Since T 00 ⊆ Q ⊆ Q(λ),
it follows from Theorem 12.14 that Qλ (T 00 ) is uniformly discrete, and from this it follows (using
Lemma 12.20) that Qλ (T 0 ) is included in the union U of a discrete set of vertical lines with a uniform
positive lower bound on interline spacing. Projecting T0 perpendicularly

   We turn now to geometry-based proofs of the cases of the dodecahedron and the icosahedron.

Proposition 14.11 (Berman & Moody [4]). The (1+ϕ)-convex closure of the regular dodecahedron
and regular icosahedron in R3 are both uniformly discrete.
                                    √
Proof. We set λ := 1 + ϕ = (3 + 5)/2. Let D be the vertices of a regular dodecahedron in R3
(its orientation and location are not important). It turns out, quite fortunately, that if we project
Qλ (D) perpendicularly into the plane containing one of D’s faces, we get a point-set equal (up to
similarity) to Qλ (P10 ), shown in Figure 11, which is discrete by Propositions 14.4 and 14.5. (Note
that λ = λ5 .)
    To see this, consider the perpendicular projection of D into the plane containing one of its
faces, shown in Figure 23. Call this projection D0 ⊆ R2 . D0 consists of two concentric decagons.
Points in the outer decagon are λ-extrapolants of pairs of adjacent points of the inner decagon; for
example, d = a ? b in the figure. Letting S be the inner decagon (a copy of P10 ), we see that all
the points of D0 are therefore in Qλ (S). Moreover, we know that Qλ (S) is uniformly discrete by
Propositions 14.4 and 14.5. Thus Qλ (D0 ) is uniformly discrete. Notice that the center point d ? c
shown in Figure 23 is also in Qλ (D0 ) (though not in D0 itself).
    As with previous arguments, we conclude that Qλ (D) is confined to the union U of a discrete
set of parallel lines in R3 with a uniform positive lower bound on interline spacing. By symmetry,
we can project onto another, nonparallel face to see that Qλ (D) is also contained in a rotated copy
of U , hence Qλ (D) must be uniformly discrete. This finishes the case of the dodecahedron.


                                                 93
     Consider the top pentagonal face of D whose projection is shown in Figure 23 centered and
bounded by solid lines. Call this face F . The center point d ? c ∈ Qλ (D0 ), shown in Figure 23, is
actually the projection of a point e ∈ R3 lying directly above F . The point e is the intersection of
lines extending the five edges of D that are incident to the vertices of F but are not edges of F itself
(their projections appear as the solid radial lines in the figure). The point e is in Qλ (D), because
e is on each of these lines, and its projection d ? c is the λ-extrapolation of points in Qλ (D0 ). By
symmetry, there are points lying similarly above the other eleven faces of D. These twelve points,
all in Qλ (D) are the vertices of a regular icosahedron I. Since I ⊆ Qλ (D), we have Qλ (I) ⊆ Qλ (D),
and thus Qλ (I) is uniformly discrete.

14.5       Other results
In this section we give some ad hoc results about specific point sets. For example, we get a lower
bound of 1/2 on the packing distances of some of the uniformly discrete sets we considered in the
last subsection. We make no attempt at generalizing our results, although such generalizations are
most likely possible.
    If λ = 2, then R2 (P6 ) = Q2 (P6 ) is a proper subset of the Eisenstein integers (the regular triangu-
lar lattice) that has both translational symmetry (in six directions) and D6 rotational symmetry14
about the center of the hexagon. For another example, if S = {0, 1, i, 1 + i} is the set of vertices
of a square (similarly positioned), then R2 (S) = Q2 (S) = Z[i], the set of Gaussian integers. If
L = {0, 1, i}, then R2 (L) is a proper subset of Z[i], however, as the following lemma shows:

Lemma 14.12. Let γ be any element of C \ Q. Then

                          Q2 ({0, 1, γ}) = {a + bγ | a, b ∈ Z & ab ≡ 0   (mod 2)} .                  (43)

If γ ∈ R, then R2 ({0, 1, γ}) = R; otherwise, R2 ({0, 1, γ}) = Q2 ({0, 1, γ}), which is a discrete set.

Proof. It is easy to check, using the fact that x ?2 y = 2y − x for any x and y, that the right-hand
side of (43) contains {0, 1, γ} and is 2-convex. This proves ⊆.
    For the reverse inclusion, we first note that Q2 ({0, 1}) = Z and Q2 ({0, γ}) = γZ, and thus
Z ∪ γZ ⊆ Q2 ({0, 1, γ}). Now let a, b ∈ Z be arbitrary. If b is even, then a + bγ = (−a) ?2 (bγ/2).
If a is even, then a + bγ = (−bγ) ?2 (a/2). In either case, this shows that a + bγ ∈ Q2 (Z ∪ γZ) =
Q2 ({0, 1, γ}), which establishes the reverse containment.
    If γ is a real, irrational number, then (43) implies that Q2 ({0, 1, γ}) is a dense subset of R, and
so R2 ({0, 1, γ}) = R. If γ ∈ / R, then Q2 ({0, 1, γ}) is a subset of the discrete lattice generated by 1
and γ.

    Considering Q2 (P6 ) again, one can easily check that P6 ⊆ Q({0, 1, η}) where η := eiτ /3 is the
principal third root of unity. Thus, by Lemma 14.12, Q2 (P6 ) = Q2 ({0, 1, η}) = {a + bη | a, b ∈ Z &
ab ≡ 0 (mod 2)}, and this is one of the sets shown in Figure 22.

    We now reconsider the set Q1+ϕ (P5 ), illustrated in Figure 10. Like Q2 (P6 ), this set is uniformly
discrete by Propositions 14.4 and 14.5 and has D5 symmetry about the center of P5 . Unlike Q2 (P6 ),
however, Q1+ϕ (P5 ) has no translational symmetry.

    Fix any a, b ∈ S such that 0 < |b − a| ≤ 1. We now show that |b − a| = 1 and a, b ∈ P . Let C
be the center of P5 , and let G be the group of rotations about C through angles that are multiples
of τ /5. (G is the 5-element cyclic group generated by ρξ,0 , where ξ := ei3τ /10 .) By symmetry, each
element of G leaves P5 , and therefore S, invariant.
Claim 14.14. There exist distinct g1 , g2 ∈ G such that

  1. {Im(g1 (a)), Im(g1 (b))} = {Im(g2 (a)), Im(g2 (b))} = {ζ, κ},

  2. the slope of the line through g1 (a) and g1 (b) is tan(τ /10), and

  3. the slope of the line through g2 (a) and g2 (b) is − tan(τ /10).

Furthermore, |b − a| = 1.

Proof of the Claim. Let θ be the argument of b − a, and let r = |b − a|. By assumption, 0 < r ≤ 1.
The elements of G rotate the line segment connecting a with b to form line segments with length
r and with arguments θ + kτ /5, for k ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4}. The vertical displacement of each such line
segment (that is, the absolute difference between the imaginary parts of the two endpoints) is thus
r| sin(θ + kτ /5)|. A simple geometric argument shows that there exist distinct k1 , k2 ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3, 4}
such that 0 < | sin(θ + k1 τ /5)| ≤ sin(τ /10) and 0 < | sin(θ + k2 τ /5)| ≤ sin(τ /10). Let g1 and g2 be
the corresponding elements of G, respectively. Now we note that
                                            √ q
                                              2      √
                              sin(τ /10) =       5 − 5 = κ − ζ ≈ 0.588 .
                                             4
We thus have

        0 < |Im(g1 (b)) − Im(g1 (a))| ≤ κ − ζ ,         0 < |Im(g2 (b)) − Im(g2 (a))| ≤ κ − ζ .     (44)

We know that g1 (a), g1 (b), g2 (a), g2 (b) ∈ S, and we established earlier that Im(S) = ρκ,ζ (Qλ ). Thus
Im(gj (a)) and Im(gj (b)) are both in ρκ,ζ (Qλ ) for j ∈ {1, 2}. Now Proposition 6.1 implies that,
except for κ and ζ, any two points of ρκ,ζ (Qλ ) differ by at least (κ − ζ)ϕ = ζ, which is strictly



                                                   95
greater than κ − ζ. This establishes the first item of the Claim, and it also implies that equality
must hold for both inequalities in (44). Therefore, for both j ∈ {1, 2},

 sin(τ /10) = κ − ζ = |Im(gj (b)) − Im(gj (a))| = r| sin(θ + kj τ /5)| ≤ | sin(θ + kj τ /5)| ≤ sin(τ /10) .

The only way this can occur is when r = 1 and | sin(θ + kj τ /5)| = sin(τ /10). Since g1 6= g2 , it must
then be that the line through g1 (a) and g1 (b) and the line through g2 (a) and g2 (b) have oppositely
signed slopes, both with absolute value tan(τ /10). By swapping g1 and g2 if necessary, we can
assume that the line through g1 (a) and g1 (b) has positive slope. This establishes the rest of the
Claim.

    It remains to show that a, b ∈ P5 , and for this it suffices that g1 (a) and g1 (b) are both in P5 .
By swapping a and b if necessary, we can assume that Im(g1 (a)) = ζ and Im(g1 (b)) = κ. To get the
slope of the line between g2 (a) and g2 (b) to be − tan(τ /10), as asserted by the Claim, it must be
that g2 results from applying g1 followed by a clockwise rotation around C through angle τ /5. Thus
we have Im(g2 (a)) = κ and Im(g2 (b)) = ζ, and this is only possible if g1 (b) = g2 (a) is the apex of P5
and g1 (a) is the element of P5 immediately to its left. This finishes the proof of Theorem 14.13.

Corollary 14.15. All R-affine transformations of C that are symmetries of Q1+ϕ (P5 ) are length-
preserving and leave P5 invariant.

    We turn to the case of the regular octagon. The (2 + 2)-clonvex closure of P8 is illustrated in

Theorem 14.16. All points in Q2+√2 (P8 ) are at least unit distance apart.

    Unlike Q1+ϕ (P5 ), Q2+√2 (P8 ) has infinitely many pairs of points that are unit distance apart;
they radiate out from P8 in the eight directions whose angles are multiples of τ /8, as can be seen
in Figure 22.

Proof√sketch of Theorem 14.16. Let S := Sλ (P8 ). Im(S) is a subset of ρκ,1 (Qλ ), where this time,
κ := 2/2. Similarly, Re(S) is a subset of ρ0,1−κ (Qλ ). Qλ is uniformly discrete by Corollary 13.5,
where m := −2 and n := 1. This makes both the real and imaginary parts of elements of S drawn
from discrete sets. Thus S is discrete.        √
    By Corollary 13.5 with λ0 = 1 − λ =  √ −1 − √2, any two√ distinct elements of Im(S) or of Re(S)
differ by at least (1 − κ)(−λ0 ) = (2 − 2)(1 + 2)/2 = 2/2. It follows that if distinct x, y ∈ S
have different real parts and different imaginary parts, then |x − y| ≥ 1. If x and y have the same
real part and different imaginary parts, or vice versa, then by symmetry we can rotate the plane
about the center of P8 through angle τ /8 to obtain images x0 and y 0 , both in S, whose real parts
and imaginary parts both differ. Then we have |x − y| = |x0 − y 0 | ≥ 1.

   We conjecture that Q2+√2 (P8 ) has no R-affine symmetries except those that leave P8 invariant.

15     Relative density of discrete Qλ (S)
Proposition 12.32 says that Rλ (S) is a subset of the model set of a certain cut-and-project scheme,
where λ is sPV and S ⊆ Z[λ] is finite. One of our chief conjectures is that these Rλ (S) are all
Meyer sets. To establish the conjecture it suffices by Fact 12.11 to show that Rλ (S) is relatively
dense (in either R or C as appropriate). While we have not succeeded in finding a general proof,
we have a number of partial results, as well as a sufficient condition for Rλ to be Meyer: This is
the case if λ is sPV and Rλ contains a unit of Z[λ] other than 1 (see Corollary 15.29).
   We begin with the case of real quadratic sPV numbers (Section 15.1), and, generalizing tech-
niques of Berman and Moody [4] and Masáková et al. [23], characterize all such numbers for which
Rλ equals a naturally corresponding model set (which is stronger than the Meyer property). As
model sets are relatively dense, relative density is immediate. We find that the model set property
                                                                          √         √           √
can only hold for four values of λ (or the corresponding 1 − λ): −ϕ, −1 − 2, −1 − 3, and −3−2 17 .
                                                                        √
We then prove it holds for each of these values; the proof for −3−2 17 is somewhat more difficult
and appears separately, as outlined in the next paragraph. We thus obtain a characterization that
strictly strengthens a previous result of Masáková et al. [23], which gave a similar characterization
for quadratic unitary Pisot numbers (a subset of quadratic sPV numbers).
    Certain aspects of Berman and Moody’s technique, which we call the covering method, are then
progressively strengthened in Section 15.2, where
                                                √     we complete the proof of our characterization
of Section 15.1 for the remaining case of −3−2 17 . Sufficient conditions for obtaining a model set
are further extended to higher degree λ. This allows us to conclude that Rλ is relatively dense
for three cubic λ’s: two non-real values, and λ7 , the latter being the appropriate parameter for
generating the set based on the regular heptagon (see Figure 12). These proofs explicitly show that
Rλ contains (but does not necessarily equal) a model set.
    Another notion, which we call affine embedding, provides a flexible tool for proving both relative
density and uniform discreteness (Section 15.3). Affine embeddings combined with the covering
method leads to the strongest sufficient condition we have for Rλ to be a Meyer set (Corollary 15.29,
as mentioned above). It also yields a method to determine if certain elements of ΣP are not
contained in Rλ , for certain λ (Section 15.3.2).
    Finally, in Section 15.4 we use the 1-dimensional (real) results outlined above to prove relative
density results in higher dimensions. Thus, for example, the relative density of Rλ7 implies the
relative density of the set of Figure 12 derived from the heptagon.

                                                   98
15.1     Relative Density in R of Rλ for Real Quadratic sPV Numbers λ
We assume throughout this subsection that λ is a real quadratic sPV number. These numbers are
characterized in Corollary 13.5.
   In Proposition 12.32, we showed that Rλ is a subset of a cut-and-project, or model, set. In the
quadratic case, according to Eq. (39) in Corollary 13.5 (for λ < 0 without loss of generality), we
can write this relation as follows:

                              Rλ ⊆ {a − bλ | a, b ∈ Z and a − bµ ∈ [0, 1]}

where µ = λ0 is the Galois conjugate of λ. (Following Berman & Moody, we use x 7→ x0 as the field
automorphism of Q(λ) = Q(µ) that swaps λ with µ.) Thus, more compactly,

                                      Rλ ⊆ {x | x ∈ Z[λ] and x0 ∈ [0, 1]}.

Following the notation of Definition 12.26, which is based in turn on the notation of Berman and
Moody [4], we denote P := [0, 1] and ΣP := {x | x ∈ Z[λ] and x0 ∈ [0, 1]}. Then the above
containment is stated simply as,
                                            R λ ⊆ ΣP .
Our goal in this section is to determine for which λ the above containment is an equality. This we
do in Theorem 15.7. When equality holds, given that ΣP is a model set, it then follows that Rλ
is relatively dense. This is one of only two ways we have been able to prove that Rλ is relatively
dense; the other is used for Corollary 15.34 later on.
    The development closely follows prior work of Berman and Moody [4] and Masáková et al. [23].
    Recall the concept of the field norm N (x) = xx0 , for x ∈ Z[λ].15 More concretely, if x = a + bλ,
then N (x) = (a + bλ)(a + bµ). It is known that N is multiplicative, and that x is a unit (has an
inverse) in Z[λ] iff N (x) = ±1.

Definition 15.1. An algebraic integer α unitary iff α is a unit of Z[α]. A quadratic integer α ∈ R
is unitary Pisot iff it is a PV number which is a unit in Z[α].

    Most quadratic sPV numbers are not unitary Pisot (as they can be irrationals with non-unit
field norm, cf. Corollary 13.5 for n > 1). In [23], Masáková et al. characterize the real quadratic
unitary Pisot numbers that lead to model sets. By a result of Pinch (in [30], Theorem 15) for real
quadratic integer α, Rα is discrete iff α is sPV. Thus real quadratic unitary Pisots can lead to
model sets only if they are also sPV. Our characterization below (Theorem 15.7) thus strengthens
that of [23], as it deals with a strict superset of those unitary Pisots that may result in model sets.
    Let Rλ0 denote the set of all conjugates of elements in Rλ . It is clear that Rλ0 = Rλ0 = Rµ .
Similarly, Σ0P = {x0 | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }. Since µ generates the same ring as λ, x ∈ Z[λ] iff
x0 ∈ Z[λ], so it is plain that Σ0P = Z[λ] ∩ P . Because the algebraic structures are the same, that
implies Rλ ⊆ ΣP iff Rµ ⊆ Z[λ] ∩ P . Further, Rλ = ΣP iff Rµ = Z[λ] ∩ P = Σ0P .
    The following result and proof is identical to an analogous one in [23]. The only observation we
make here is that it’s not necessary to assume that λ is unitary Pisot.

Lemma 15.2. If Rµ = Σ0P , then for any y ∈ Σ0P , both of the following hold:
  15

x is an algebraic integer, then N (x) is also an integer.

 (i) µ | y or µ | (y − 1).
 (ii) (1 − µ) | y or (1 − µ) | (y − 1).
(Divisibility is with respect to Z[λ] = Z[µ].)

same, but based on the i = n term in the sum.

of a fundamental unit ud ∈ Z[ d], it follows that the fundamental
                                                          √             unit uD of Z[ D] is a power of
ud > 1: for some k > 0, uD = ukd , and the units of Z[ D] are powers of uD . This fact is used in
the next proof.
    Given these considerations, in the next lemma we need only rely on the existence of arbitrarily
large units in Z[λ], which usually coincide with neither λ nor 1 − λ (so that λ need not be unitary).
Otherwise the result and the proof are essentially those of a similar lemma in [23].
Lemma 15.3. Let λ be a real quadratic sPV number. Suppose that Rµ = Σ0P . Then in the ring
Z[λ], µ | 2 and (1 − µ) | 2.
Proof. For any unit z ∈ Z[λ], we know that it has an inverse z −1 ∈ Z[λ] so that zz −1 = 1. We may
take z to be a power of a fundamental unit > 1, and z −1 > 0. Since z, z −1 ∈ R and z > 1, the
equation zz −1 = 1 then implies that z −1 ∈ (0, 1). Furthermore, we choose z sufficiently large that
z −1 ∈ (0, 1/2). Let u = z −1 denote this unit. Thus u, 2u ∈ Z[λ] ∩ P = Σ0P , and Lemma 15.2 can
be applied to y = u and y = 2u.
    If µ is a unit, then µ | 2 and we are done. Suppose then that µ is not a unit, and that µ - 2.
Since µ is not a unit, we also have µ - u. Now if µ | 2u, given that u is a unit, we would have
2 = µyu−1 , u−1 ∈ Z[λ], for some y ∈ Z[λ], so that µ | 2, a contradiction. Thus µ - 2 and µ - u also
imply that µ - 2u.
    Since µ - 2u, by Lemma 15.2 part (i), µ | (2u − 1). From the fact that µ - u we conclude from
the same Lemma that µ | (u − 1). Then µ | (2u − 1 − u + 1), i.e., µ | u, a contradiction. Hence µ | 2.
    The exact same argument applies to 1 − µ, appealing to part (ii) of Lemma 15.2.

Lemma 15.4. For any real quadratic sPV number λ such that in the ring Z[λ], µ | 2 and (1 − µ) | 2
(equivalently, λ | 2 and (1 − λ) | 2), one of the following must hold:

                                                  100
(a) λ = −ϕ or λ = 1 + ϕ
            √            √
(b) λ = −1 − 2 or λ = 2 + 2
            √            √
(c) λ = −1 − 3 or λ = 2 + 3
            √              √
(d) λ = −3−2 17 or λ = 5+2 17

Proof. We consider λ < 0; the postive case is similar. Then by Corollary 13.5, λ and µ have
the minimal polynomial x2 + mx − n, for 0 < n ≤ m. Since µ | 2, there are a, b ∈ Z such that
(a + bµ)µ = 2. Given that µ2 + mµ − n = 0, a little calculation gives the constraint nb = 2. This
is only possible if n = 1 or n = 2.
    Since we also have (1 − µ) | 2, there are c, d ∈ Z such that (c + dµ)(1 − µ) = 2. Again, a little
                           2
calculation yields d = m−n+1   . Since d ∈ Z, this can only hold if m − n = 0 or 1.
    If n = 1, we have the unitary Pisot case, which is essentially treated in [23]. For the sake of
completeness, we give the argument here: From n = 1 and the constraint m − n = 0 or 1, we
          √ m = 1 or m = 2. In the first case we obtain λ = −ϕ (item (a)), and in the second,
conclude that
λ = −1 − 2 (item (b)).                                                                    √
    Now suppose n = 2. Then m = 2 or m =√3. The former case corresponds to λ = −1 − 3, item
(c). The latter case corresponds to λ = −3−2 17 , item (d).

   From Lemmas 15.3 and 15.4 we immediately get the following:

Lemma 15.5. For any real quadratic sPV number λ such that Rµ = Σ0P (equivalently, Rλ = ΣP ),
one of the items (a), (b), (c), (d) of Lemma 15.4 must hold.

    In each of the items (a), (b), and (c) in Lemma 15.4, at least one of the two alternative values
of λ is unitary Pisot. We next prove that Rλ = ΣP for these values of λ. This was first proved
in [23] using the notion of β-expansions due to Rényi [31]. Here we include a self-contained proof
that does not use β-expansions. The original method of Berman and Moody [4] can only be applied
directly to λ = −ϕ or 1 + ϕ. We extend the technique for the other two values.

Theorem 15.6 (Masáková et al. [23]). If λ or 1 − λ is as in Lemma 15.4 items (a), (b), or (c),
then Rλ = ΣP .

Proof. We find it convenient to have µ > 1/2. This can always be arranged either by using λ or
1 − λ, and since Rλ = R1−λ , there is no loss of generality. Furthermore, whenever we make this
choice for the relevant
                √               √ 15.4), then 1 − λ is a unit. To be concrete, this dictates that
                        λ’s (Lemma
we use λ = 2 + 2 or λ = −1 − 3. In those cases, λ is not a unit. Only when λ = 1 + ϕ does it
hold that both λ and 1 − λ are units; that is what makes that case easier.
   For any set A, define x ? A = {x ? a | a ∈ A}, and similarly A ? x = {a ? x | a ∈ A}.
   Our primary task is to prove that

                            ΣP = 0 ? ΣP ∪ 1 ? ΣP ∪ ΣP ? 0 ∪ ΣP ? 1.                             (46)

This is a generalization of Berman and Moody’s “replication” property. It says that any element
in ΣP can be obtained by extrapolation on the left or the right by 0 or 1 with another element in
ΣP . The main result then follows easily, as explained below.


                                                101
   We compute 0 ? ΣP , 1 ? ΣP , ΣP ? 0, and ΣP ? 1 in turn, starting with 0 ? ΣP :

                             0 ? ΣP   = {0 ? x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                                      = {λx | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                                      = {λx | λx ∈ λZ[λ] ∧ (λx)0 ∈ µP }

Note at this point that, because λ is not necessarily a unit (unless λ = −ϕ or 1 + ϕ), the ideal
λZ[λ] is not equal to the ring Z[λ]. We address this issue later. After changing variables λx 7→ x,

                                0 ? ΣP   = {x | x ∈ λZ[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ µP }.                         (47)

Note the important fact that µP = [0, µ].
   Proceeding similarly,

      1 ? ΣP     = {1 ? x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                 = {1 − λ + λx | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                 = {1 − λ + λx | 1 − λ + λx ∈ 1 − λ + λZ[λ] ∧ (1 − λ + λx)0 ∈ 1 − µ + µP },

and hence,

                       1 ? ΣP = {x | x ∈ 1 − λ + λZ[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ 1 − µ + µP }.                    (48)

   Observe that 1 − µ + µP = [1 − µ, 1]. Further note that, because µ > 1/2,

                       µP ∪ (1 − µ + µP ) = [0, µ] ∪ [1 − µ, 1] = [0, 1] = P.

It is crucial that the intervals µP and 1 − µ + µP cover P .
    Next we extrapolate ΣP on the right with 0:

               ΣP ? 0 = {x ? 0 | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                       = {(1 − λ)x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
                       = {(1 − λ)x | (1 − λ)x ∈ (1 − λ)Z[λ] ∧ ((1 − λ)x)0 ∈ (1 − µ)P }.

Because 1 − λ is a unit, we have (1 − λ)Z[λ] = Z[λ]. Therefore,

                             ΣP ? 0 = {x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ (1 − µ)P }.                         (49)

   Observe that (1 − µ)P = [0, 1 − µ] is a proper subset of [0, 1/2] because of µ > 1/2.
   Finally we extrapolate ΣP on the right with 1:

  ΣP ? 1 = {x ? 1 | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
             = {λ + (1 − λ)x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ P }
             = {λ + (1 − λ)x | λ + (1 − λ)x ∈ λ + (1 − λ)Z[λ] ∧ (λ + (1 − λ)x)0 ∈ µ + (1 − µ)P }.

Using the fact that λ + (1 − λ)Z[λ] = Z[λ],

                           ΣP ? 1 = {x | x ∈ Z[λ] ∧ x0 ∈ µ + (1 − µ)P },                       (50)


                                                  102
Observe that µ + (1 − µ)P = [µ, 1]. Further note that, because µ > 1/2,

                  (1 − µ)P ∪ (µ + (1 − µ)P ) = [0, 1 − µ] ∪ [µ, 1] = P \ (1 − µ, µ).

The fact that (1 − µ)P and µ + (1 − µ)P fail to cover P in general necessitates extrapolation on
the left as well as the right.
    We now address the nature of the ideal λZ[λ]. From Lemma 15.3, we know that λ | 2. As
mentioned previously, λ is not a unit, unless λ = 1 + ϕ or −ϕ. When λ = 1 + ϕ or −ϕ, λ is a unit,
so λZ[λ] = Z[λ],
              √ and the problem goes away (more    √ on this below). But in the other cases N (λ) 6= 1:
For λ = 2 + 2, N (λ) = 2, and for λ = −1 − 3, N (λ) = −2. Now for any x = a + bλ ∈ Z[λ], we
have x = a + bλ ≡ a (mod λ). That is, x ≡ k (mod λ) where k is an integer16 . However, because
λ | 2, we find that 2 ≡ 0 (mod λ). Hence for any x ∈ Z[λ], we have either x ≡ 0 (mod λ) or
x ≡ 1 (mod λ). Hence the ideal λZ[λ] partitions Z[λ] into two classes, one equivalent to 0 mod
λ, one equivalent to 1 mod λ, and preserving addition and multiplication when N (λ) = ±2. Then
Z[λ]/λZ[λ] ∼ = Z2 . As this is a field, λZ[λ] is a maximal ideal; it is also possible to prove the latter
fact directly. However, in this proof we really only need the partition of Z[λ] into the “0” class and
the “1” class.
    The fact that λZ[λ] induces this partitioning (for λ 6= −ϕ or 1 + ϕ) leads to the necessity of
extrapolating on the right as well as the left.
    We now turn to the task of establishing Eq. (46). First, because each is a subset of Z[λ], and
the relevant convex sets (e.g., P , µP , etc.) are contained in P , it is clear that each of 0 ? ΣP , 1 ? ΣP ,
ΣP ? 0, ΣP ? 1 are contained in ΣP . For the reverse containment, consider any x ∈ ΣP . There are
three cases:
  (i) x0 ∈ [0, 1 − µ]: By Eq. (49), ΣP ? 0 contains all x ∈ Z[λ] such that x0 ∈ [0, 1 − µ]. Thus this
      implies that x ∈ ΣP ? 0.

 (ii) x0 ∈ [µ, 1]: By Eq. (50), ΣP ? 1 contains all x ∈ Z[λ] such that x0 ∈ [µ, 1]. Thus this implies
      that x ∈ ΣP ? 1.

(iii) x0 ∈ (1 − µ, µ): By the partition of Z[λ], we have that either x ≡ 0 (mod λ) or x ≡ 1
      (mod λ). If the former, then x ∈ λZ[λ], and hence (since x0 ∈ µP = [0, µ]), by Eq. (47), we
      have x ∈ 0 ? ΣP . If the latter, then x ∈ 1 − λ + λZ[λ], and hence (since x0 ∈ 1 − µ + µP =
      [1 − µ, 1]), by Eq. (48), we have x ∈ 1 ? ΣP .
This establishes Eq. (46). If λ = 1 + ϕ, we can take µ < 1/2, and we would only have cases (i) and
(ii) above. That is, for λ = 1 + ϕ, we find ΣP = ΣP ? 0 ∪ ΣP ? 1.                               √
           √ is completed by an induction to show that ΣP ⊆ Rλ . We first consider λ = 2 + 2

all of which are in Rλ .

    The foregoing proof requires either λ or 1 − λ to be a unit. This does not hold for λ = −3−2 17 ,
and hence this requires a different technique (although still not
                                                               √

in Section 15.2. It turns out that Rλ = ΣP for λ = −3−2 17 as well (see Proposition 15.18 in

is immediate:
Theorem 15.7. If λ is a real quadratic sPV number, then Rλ = ΣP if and only if λ is one of the
cases (a), (b), (c), or (d) of Lemma 15.4.
   This can be further strengthened. In [30, Theorem 15], Pinch shows that if λ is real and not
an integer, and Rλ is discrete, then λ has a conjugate in (0, 1). Hence if λ is real and quadratic
and Rλ is discrete, we can conclude that λ is sPV. Conversely, we know that if λ is sPV, then
Rλ is discrete. Thus for real, quadratic λ, Rλ is discrete iff λ is sPV. We thus have the following
corollary of Theorem 15.7 (recall that if λ ∈ R and Rλ is discrete, then λ is an algebraic integer by
Theorem 8.1):
Corollary 15.8. If λ is real and quadratic, and Rλ is discrete, then Rλ = ΣP if and only if λ is
one of the cases (a), (b), (c), or (d) of Lemma 15.4.
    As per the discussion just after Definition 15.1, Theorem 15.7 also implies the following imme-
diately:
Corollary 15.9 (Masáková et al. [23]). If λ is unitary Pisot, then Rλ = ΣP iff λ is one of the
cases (a), (b), and (c) of Lemma 15.4.

                                                   104
15.2       The Covering Method and its Applications
The underlying strategy of the proof of Theorem 15.6 is to find a finite cover of the interval P = [0, 1]
(in that case, simply the intervals [0, 1 − µ], [1 − µ, µ], [µ, 1]), such that for any x ∈ ΣP , depending
on which element of the cover x0 is contained in, we can write x as b ?λ y or y ?λ b, for b ∈ {0, 1}.
Since, in all of these four cases, |y| < |x|, an inductive proof then suffices to show that ΣP ⊆ Rλ .
    In this section we generalize that proof,
                                            √     which yields further relative density results, including
                                        −3− 17
for case (d) of Theorem 15.7, λ =          2   . That case is covered in Proposition 15.18 below.
    In particular, the technique allows us to use a fixed set of “seed” points other than {0, 1}.
Indeed, it turns out that when ΣP has a unit, Theorem 15.10 below shows that it is always possible
to adjoin finitely many points Y in ΣP to Rλ such that it equals ΣP , i.e., for some Y ⊆ ΣP ,
ΣP = Rλ (Y ). Thus, if we can explicitly find such a set Y that is a subset of Rλ , then Rλ itself
is cut and project: ΣP = Rλ . However, the result yields more than relative density results. As
we will see later on, even when Y 6⊆ Rλ , the results of this section enable us to prove that certain
elements of ΣP are not in Rλ , again in certain cases.
    The proof of Theorem 15.10 extends that of Theorem 15.6 in two ways. First, the size of the
cover of P is larger, but still finite (depending on the size of the fundamental unit in ΣP ). Secondly,
the proof that ΣP ⊆ Rλ proceeds by inducting on |z| as before, but now a larger finite set Y of
seed points plays the role that {0, 1} played in the proof of Theorem 15.6. In one respect, the
induction is simpler, since we find z can be written as x ?λ y where x ∈ Y and hence (as shown
below) |z| > |y|. That is, z is the extrapolant of one of a finite number of seed points on the left
only with a smaller point on the right.

15.2.1      Quadratics and Non-Real Cubics
We begin by considering quadratic sPV numbers and non-real cubic (degree 3) sPV numbers. In
the latter case—as with the real quadratic case—λ has a unique conjugate µ ∈ (0, 1). It also has
λ∗ as its other conjugate. In this section, we again let x 7→ x0 be the unique field isomorphism17
Q(λ) → Q(µ) that maps λ to µ, and we define P := [0, 1] and ΣP = Σ[0,1] := {x ∈ Z[λ] | x0 ∈ [0, 1]}
as before. So if λ ∈
                   / R, we have

                        ΣP = {a + bλ + cλ2 | a, b, c ∈ Z & 0 ≤ a + bµ + cµ2 ≤ 1} .                           (51)

Since ΣP is a cut-and-project (model) set, it is relatively dense in C ∼
                                                                       = R2 .
    The next theorem shows that, for any sPV λ with a unique conjugate in (0, 1), if there exists
an α ∈ ΣP that is a unit of Z[λ] other than 1, then there exists a finite set Y ⊆ ΣP such that
Rα (Y ) = ΣP . If, in addition, α ∈ Rλ , then Rλ (Y ) = ΣP . The theorem also gives sufficient
conditions on Y such that Rα (Y ) = ΣP . These conditions are sometimes not completely necessary,
however, as one can often get
                            √ by with a smaller “seed” set Y . This will be true, for example, in
the case where λ = −(3 + 13)/2 (Proposition 15.32, below). This theorem is our main tool for
proving relative density of Rλ for such λ.

1/qk+1 for all k ≥ 1.
   Then we have

                             Σ0P = {1} ∪ {dbµe − bµ | b ∈ Z} = {1} ∪ {{aµ} | a ∈ Z} ,

where {aµ} := aµ − baµc is the fractional part of aµ. (To see this, set a := −b.) For integer n ≥ 0,
let
                    Xn := {1} ∪ {dbµe − bλ | b ∈ Z & −n ≤ b ≤ 0} ⊆ ΣP .
Then Xn0 = {1} ∪ {{aµ} | a ∈ Z & 0 ≤ a ≤ n}. Much is known about sets of this form for n ≥ 0.
By the three-distance theorem (see [2, Section 2.6]), there are at most three possible distances
between adjacent points of Xn0 —the smallest distance is ηk , where k ≥ −1 is largest such that
qk ≤ n [2, Theorem 2.6.2]; the largest distance dn satisfies the bound

                                                       dn ≤ ηk + ηk−1 ,                                   (57)

where k ≥ 0 is largest such that qk ≤ n + 1 [2, Theorem 2.6.3].
     We now consider choosing X to be Xn for n sufficiently large so that Equation (52) is satisfied.
The intervals on the right cover [0, 1] just when there are no gaps between adjacent intervals. That
is, for any x, y ∈ Xn such that x0 < y 0 are adjacent in Xn0 , is suffices that (1 − β)x0 + β ≥ (1 − β)y 0 ,
  19
       Generally speaking, a0 = bµc and µ = a0 + 1/[a1 , a2 , . . .], etc.




                                                               107
or equivalently, y 0 − x0 ≤ β/(1 − β). Thus it suffices to choose n so that dn ≤ β/(1 − β), and by
Equation (57) this will be true if
                                                        β
                                        ηk + ηk−1 ≤         ,                                 (58)
                                                      1−β
where k ≥ 0 is largest such that qk ≤ n + 1. Thus we first choose the least k satisfying (58), then
we set n := qk − 1.
     Letting X be Xn , although sufficient, is not an optimal choice, because it does not minimize
maxx∈X |x| and thus the value of M in Equation (53). To minimize M , notice that any set of the
form Wm,n := {1} ∪ {dbµe − bλ | b ∈ Z & m ≤ b ≤ m + n} for m ∈ Z ∩ [−n, 0] works just as well as
Xn in covering [0, 1]. This is because Wm,n is the image of Xn under the cyclic shift permutation
c : [0, 1] → [0, 1] defined for x ∈ [0, 1] by
                                          
                                             1                if x = 1,
                                 c(x) =
                                             (x + {mµ}) mod 1 if x < 1.

which preserves the set of distances between adjacent points in Xn versus those in Wm,n . This
means that, given our choice of n above, we can set X := Wm,n where m minimizes maxx∈X |x| =
maxx∈Wm,n |x|. If λ > 0, then one can verify that m = d−n/2e, and likewise if λ < 0, then
m = b−n/2c. In the former case,
                          
                             λn/2 − bµn/2c               if n is even and n > 0,
                max |x| =                                                                 (59)
                x∈X          λ(n + 1)/2 − dµ(n + 1)/2e if n is odd.

In the latter case,
                             
                                 dµn/2e − λn/2             if n is even and n > 0,
                 max |x| =                                                                     (60)
                  x∈X            bµ(n + 1)/2c − λ(n + 1)/2 if n is odd.

(Verifying the above is made easier by noting that λ ∈
                                                     / [−1, 2].) In either case, if n = 0, then
Wm,n = {0, 1}, so maxx∈X |x| = 1.
   We summarize the foregoing in the following proposition.

Proposition 15.11. In Theorem 15.10 where λ is real quadratic, it suffices to let

                        X := {1} ∪ {dbµe − bλ | b ∈ Z & m ≤ b ≤ m + n} ,

where n = qk − 1 for the least k satisfying (58) for ηk defined by (56) and qk defined by (55) based
on the continued fraction expansion of λ0 , and m = d−n/2e if λ > 0 and m = b−n/2c otherwise.
For this X, the value maxx∈X |x| is given by Equation (59) or (60), provided n > 0.

15.2.2    Higher Degrees
Theorem 15.10 is specific to quadratic sPV’s or non-real sPV’s of degree 3. In this section we turn
to real sPV’s of arbitrary degree, real or complex.
    For any subset R of a topological space, we let Rint denote the interior of R.
    Fix a strong PV number λ with k many conjugates 0 < µ0 < µ1 < · · · < µk−1 < 1 in (0, 1).
Here we assume that λ is nontrivial, so that k ≥ 1. (We handle the case of trivial sPV λ in
Proposition 15.46, below). For any z ∈ Rk and 0 ≤ i < k, we let zi be the (i + 1)st component of z.

                                                  108
Particularly, for any x ∈ Z[λ] and 0 ≤ i < k, we let x0i denote the conjugate of x that is the image of
x under the unique ring homomorphism Z[λ] → Z[µi ] mapping λ to µi (cf. Definition 12.26), and so
we let x0 := (x00 , x01 , . . . , x0k−1 ), viewed as a column vector. We also let Λx := diag(x00 , x01 , . . . , x0k−1 ),
the diagonal matrix with the x0i ’s along the diagonal. Set P := [0, 1]×k and for any S ⊆ Rk let ΣS
          (λ)
denote ΣS , recalling that this is {x ∈ Z[λ] | x0 ∈ S}.
    The following generalizes Theorem 15.10, but with any sub-box S of P that can be arbitrarily
close to, but never equals, P .

Theorem 15.12. Suppose ΣP contains an α 6= 1 that is a unit of Z[λ]. Fix any 0 < ε < 1/2, and
define S := [ε, 1 − ε]×k . There exists a finite set Y ⊆ ΣP such that ΣS ⊆ Qα (Y ). If, in addition,
α ∈ Qλ , then ΣS ⊆ Qλ (Y ).

Proof. This proof follows the general approach of that of Theorem 15.10. Since α ∈ ΣP , we have
0 < αi0 < 1 for all 0 ≤ i < k. Since α is a unit, 1/α ∈ Z[λ], and (1/α)0 = (1/α00 , 1/α10 , . . . , 1/αk0 ).
Set Λ := Λα = diag(α00 , . . . , αk−1
                                  0   ). For any x ∈ Rk , define

               Jx := x ?Λ S = {z ∈ Rk | (∃y ∈ S) z = x ?Λ y} = {z ∈ Rk | x ?Λ−1 z ∈ S} .

For the last equality, one can readily check that for all x, y, z ∈ Rk , if x ?Λ y = z then y = x ?Λ−1 z
and conversely.

Claim 15.14. Jz−1 ∩ P int 6= ∅ for all z ∈ S.

Proof. It suffices to show for every 0 ≤ i < k that if ε ≤ zi ≤ 1 − ε, then

                           (1 − ε) ?1/(1−α0i ) zi < 1 and ε ?1/(1−α0i ) zi > 0 .

Fixing i and letting β := αi0 , we get the following chain of equivalences for the first inequality above:
                                           
                                        1                  1
                                  1−           (1 − ε) +      zi < 1
                                      1−β                1−β
                                                   zi − β(1 − ε) < 1 − β
                                                                      zi < 1 − βε ,

and the last inequality is certainly true if zi ≤ 1 − ε (because β < 1). Similarly, for the second
inequality:
                                             
                                          1           1
                                    1−          ε+        zi > 0
                                        1−β         1−β
                                                    zi − βε > 0
                                                                   zi > βε ,

which is again true if zi ≥ ε.

   We know that Σ0P is dense in P (and in fact, Q0α = QΛ ({0, 1}) is dense in P by Lemma 12.22).
For every z ∈ S, choose an element xz of Jz−1 ∩ Σ0P in some standard way. Such an element exists
by density and because Jz−1 ∩ P int is open and nonempty by Claim 15.14. Then z ∈ Jxz int for all
z ∈ S, meaning that the family {Jxz int | z ∈ S} is an open cover of S. Since S is compact, we may
choose a finite subcover, indexed by a finite set X ⊆ ΣP so that {Jx0 int | x ∈ X} covers S.

Remark. It is only necessary that S be covered by the Jx0 themselves, not their interiors. The
interiors were only used to apply the compactness argument. Thus one may be able to find a
smaller set X such that the Jx0 cover S.                                                    
   The rest of the proof is very close to that of Theorem 15.10, with only some subtle differences.
An argument similar to the one used in the proof of that theorem shows that |α| > 1. Let

                                    Y := X ∪ {y ∈ ΣS : |y| ≤ M } ,

where
                                                  |α − 1|

                                                  |α| − 1       x∈X

Y is certainly finite, because ΣP is uniformly discrete. We now apply Lemma 15.15, given below,
to obtain the first part of the theorem.
    If α ∈ Qλ , then Qα (Y ) ⊆ Qλ (Y ) by Lemma 2.25, and the rest of the theorem follows.


                                                     110
Remark. There is a possible trade-off between the choice of X versus Y . It is not necessary that

possible by Lemma 12.22), then we can just let Y := {y ∈ ΣS : |y| ≤ M } (albeit with a possibly
bigger value of M ).                                                                           

Lemma 15.15. Let λ, P , S, α, and k be as in Theorem 15.12. Suppose there exists a finite set
X ⊆ Z[λ] such that, setting Λ := diag(α00 , . . . , αk−1
                                                     0   ) and Jx0 := x0 ?Λ S for each x ∈ X, it holds that
    S                                                              |α−1|
S⊆     Jx0 . Let Y = X ∪ {y ∈ ΣS : |y| ≤ M }, where M = |α|−1            maxx∈X |x|. Then ΣS ⊆ Qα (Y ).
    x∈X

Proof. We show for any z ∈ ΣS that z is in Qα (Y ). We do this by induction on |z| as in Theo-
rem 15.10. The only difference here is that, due to the arbitrary degree of λ, there are multiple
conjugates. If |z| ≤ M , then z ∈ Y ⊆ Qα (Y ) and we are done, so suppose z ∈ ΣS with |z| > M .
By definition of ΣS , z 0 ∈ S. Since {Jx0 |x ∈ X} covers S, for some x ∈ X we have z 0 ∈ Jx0 . Let
y := x ?1/α z, so that x ?α y = z. As before, since α is a unit in Z[λ], we have y ∈ Z[λ]. We also
have that |y| < |z| by an argument identical to the one for Theorem 15.10, repeated here in more
detail:
                                                  
                                                1         1
                               |y| =       1−         x+ z
                                                α         α
                                              1           |z|
                                   ≤ 1−           · |x| +
                                              α           |α|
                                        1

                                       |α|
                                                         |α| − 1
                                                                        
                                        1
                                   ≤          |α − 1| ·          M + |z|
                                       |α|               |α − 1|
                                        1

                                       |α|
                                        1
                                   <        ((|α| − 1)|z| + |z|)
                                       |α|
                                   = |z|.

    It remains to show that y 0 ∈ S, thus putting y in ΣS . This suffices: applying the inductive
hypothesis to y to get y ∈ Qα (Y ), and given that x ∈ Y ⊆ Qα (Y ), we get z = x ?α y ∈ Qα (Y ) as
desired. We have
                                    y 0 = (x ?1/α z)0 = x0 ?Λ−1 z 0 .
Since z 0 ∈ Jx0 , we then have y 0 ∈ x0 ?Λ−1 Jx0 = x0 ?Λ−1 (x0 ?Λ S) = S by the definition of Jx0 and a
simple calculation. We conclude by induction that ΣS ⊆ Qα (Y ).

Proposition 15.16. Let λ be the root of the polynomial x3 + x2 − 1 closest to the point −0.877 +
0.745i (see Figure 9). Then Rλ = ΣP .

Proof. We have that λ is cubic sPV with conjugate µ ≈ 0.754878. (The exact form of µ is not
important.) As in other cases, λ is itself a unit of Z[λ], so we set α := λ whence β = µ. Since

Y be as in Equation (54), we get Rλ (Y ) = ΣP . Thus it suffices to show that Y ⊆ Rλ .

                          ΣP = {1} ∪ {dbµ + cµ2 e − bλ − cλ2 | b, c ∈ Z} .

Proposition 15.18. Let λ := −(3 + 17)/2. Then Qλ = ΣP , where P = [0, 1].

Proof. The minimal polynomial of λ and its conjugate µ := ( 17 − 3)/2 is x2 + 3x − 2. In this
case, λ is not a unit of Z[λ], but Qλ contains the value α := 9 − 16λ ≈ 65.9848, which is a unit of

    We will apply Proposition 15.11 in this case. We start with the continued fraction expansion
of µ, which is [a0 , a1 , a2 , . . .] = [0, 1, 1, 3], where the bar indicates that the pattern 1, 1, 3 repeats
forever. The table below gives the values needed to calculate n and m in that proposition. To get
pk and qk for k ≥ 0, we can use the recurrences pk = ak pk−1 + pk−2 and qk = ak qk−1 + qk−2 . The
values of pk and qk for k < 0 are given in order to obtain the correct initial values when k = 0 and
k = 1. Recall that ηk = (−1)k (qk µ − pk ).

             k     −2 −1     0     1      2      3      4       5        6        7
             ak              0     1      1      3      1       1        3        1
             pk     0      1 0     1      1      4      5       9       32       41
             qk     1      0 1     1      2      7      9      16       57       73
             ηk     µ      1 µ 1 − µ 2µ − 1 4 − 7µ 9µ − 5 9 − 16µ 57µ − 32 41 − 73µ

From the table, we get that η7 + η6 = 9 − 16µ = β < β/(1 − β), so k = 7 satisfies Equation (58).
Furthermore, this is the least such k-value: 0.023665 ≈ η6 +η5 = 41µ−23 > β/(1−β) = (2µ−1)/8 ≈

                                x∈X

                                                           113
15.2.5    Case Study: λ7
Recall Proposition 14.5, in which we characterize those λn (Eq. (42)) that are sPV (thus giving
rise to uniformly discrete Qλn ), for odd n. We know that Qλ3 and Qλ5 are also relatively dense. In
this section we use Theorem 15.12 to prove that Qλ7 is also relatively dense.
    For the remainder of this section let λ denote λ7 ≈ 5.0489, whose minimal polynomial is
x3 − 6x2 + 5x − 1. Denote the conjugates of λ by µ, ν ∈ [0, 1], where µ ≈ 0.30798 and ν ≈ 0.64310.
For x ∈ Z[λ], where x is of the form a0 + a1 λ + a2 λ2 for a0 , a1 , a2 ∈ Z, we denote its conjugates
by x0 = a0 + a1 µ + a2 µ2 and x00 = a0 + a1 ν + a2 ν 2 . By Fact 12.29, we have Qλ ⊆ ΣP , where
P = [0, 1]×2 . Let S ⊆ P denote the (2ν − 1) × (2ν − 1) square with corners at (1 − ν, 1 − ν) and
(ν, ν). It is convenient to introduce the parallelogram T with corners at (0, 0), (µ, ν), (1 − µ, 1 − ν),
and (1, 1). It is easy to see that S ⊆ T ⊆ P and hence ΣS ⊆ ΣT ⊆ ΣP . We have,

                                    ΣT = {x ∈ Z[λ] | (x0 , x00 ) ∈ T }.

We can characterize the points of ΣT as follows. Let β = µ + ν − µν and define

                                     n(m) := dm/λe = dµνme
                                     `(m) := bβmc + n(m)
                                     p(m) := n(m) − `(m)λ + mλ2 .

Proposition 15.19. ΣT = {1, λ, 1 − λ} ∪ {p(m) | m ∈ Z}.

Proof. It is easiest to see this by transforming P via the matrix W defined in Fact 12.29. In this
instance,
                                                                          
                           1 µ                          −1    1     ν −µ
                   W =              ,                W =                       .
                           1 ν                              ν − µ −1 1

We interpret the column vector [x0 , x00 ]T as the point (x0 , x00 ). Let Ω = W −1 T . It is easy to see
that W −1 maps (0, 0) 7→ (0, 0), (µ, ν) 7→ (0, 1), (1, 1) 7→ (1, 0), and (1 − µ, 1 − ν) 7→ (1, −1). Thus
Ω is a parallelogram with corners at (0, 0), (0, 1), (1, 0), and (1, −1). More generally, W −1 maps
(x0 , x00 ) = (a0 + a1 µ + a2 µ2 , a0 + a1 ν + a2 ν 2 ) to (a0 − µνa2 , a1 + (µ + ν)a2 ). Thus (x0 , x00 ) ∈ T iff
the following conditions hold:

                          0      ≤   a0 − µνa2    ≤        1
                                                                                                             (61)
                      −a0 + µνa2 ≤ a1 + (µ + ν)a2 ≤ −a0 + µνa2 + 1

Note that for a2 6= 0, all inequalities above are proper because µ and ν are irrational. The
first inequality shows that a0 = dµνa2 e, and the second a1 = −dµνa2 e + dµν − (µ + ν)a2 e =
−dµνa2 e − bβa2 c. Letting m denote a2 , this yields a0 = n(m) and a1 = −`(m). Thus the points in
ΣT are of the form p(m), m ∈ Z. The exceptions are the nonzero corners of T , namely (1, 0), (0, 1)
and (1, −1), which correspond to the values 1, λ and 1 − λ.

Remark. The apparent “outlier” nature of the points 1, λ, and 1−λ is an artifact of the definition
of p(m), which has a λ2 term for any m 6= 0. In that case, the inequalities in Eq. (61) are proper.
However, for m = a2 = 0, the equalities can be met, which accounts for the points 1, λ, and 1 − λ.


                                                       114
The one value of p(m) that works out for one of the initial points is p(0), all of whose terms are 0.


   We find it is possible to choose a finite number of points in ΣT to construct the set Y of Theo-
rem 15.12. We remark that all the points we use are in T \ S.

    We now prove the main result of this section. Since λ is a unit, by Theorem 15.12, there exists
a finite Y ⊆ ΣP such that ΣS ⊆ Qλ (Y ). Our task here is to explicitly construct such a Y such that
Y ⊆ Qλ . That, in turn, requires finding an explicit finite cover of the set S, indexed by a finite set
X. Once X is found, the problem reduces to the tedious but routine verification that Y ⊆ Qλ , and
finally appealing to Lemma 15.15.
Lemma 15.20. ΣS ⊆ Qλ .
Proof. We adopt the notations and conventions of the proof of Theorem 15.12. We construct the
set Y and show that Y ⊆ Qλ . Since Qλ (Y ) = Qλ , the result follows easily by Theorem 15.12.
    Let X = {λ, 1 − λ} ∪ {p(m)|m = ±12, ±13, ±16, ±17}. We claim that C = {Jx |x ∈ X} forms a

15.3     Affine Embedding
15.3.1    Definitions and General Results
Recall that for x, y ∈ C, the map ρx,y : C → C maps z ∈ C to x ?z y (Definition 2.6). This definition
extends naturally to any module over a commutative ring (Definition 12.15).

Definition 15.22. Let R be a commutative ring and A, B subsets of R. If ρa,b (A) ⊆ B for some
a, b ∈ R such that x(a − b) 6= 0 for all x ∈ R \ {0}, then we say A affinely embeds in B, and write
A ,→ B. If A ,→ B and B ,→ A, we say A and B are affine-equivalent, and write A         B.

    The condition on a and b in Definition 15.22 is equivalent to ρa,b being one-to-one, hence the
use of the term “embedding.” If R has no zero-divisors, then ρa,b is a one-to-one map just when
a 6= b. We will only apply Definition 15.22 when R has no zero-divisors.
    The ,→ relation is clearly reflexive and transitive (cf. Fact 2.7(4) for R = C), making     an
equivalence relation.

Fact 15.23. Let F be either R or C. Let A and B be subsets of F such that A ,→ B.

  1. If B is uniformly discrete, then A is uniformly discrete.

  2. If A is relatively dense in F , then B is relatively dense in F .

  3. For any λ ∈ F , Qλ (A) ,→ Qλ (B) and Rλ (A) ,→ Rλ (B). (This follows from Lemma 2.12.)

Lemma 15.24. Let T be any finite subset of Q[x]. Then T ,→ Q[x] . Furthermore, there exist
distinct a, b ∈ Z[x] such that ρa,b (T ) ⊆ Q[x] and the only irreducible monic polynomials in Q[x]
dividing b − a are x and x − 1.




                                                 117
Proof. Suppose T = {f1 , . . . , fn }, where fi ∈ Q[x] for each 1 ≤ i ≤ n. Choose d, `, k ∈ Z+
sufficiently large that for all 1 ≤ i ≤ n, dfi ∈ Z[x] and (dfi (λ) + `) · λk (1 − λ)k ∈ (0, 1) for any
λ ∈ (0, 1). Then, writing h(x) := xk (1 − x)k and gi := (dfi + `) · h, we find that gi ∈ Z[x] and
gi (λ) ∈ (0, 1) for any λ ∈ (0, 1). By the characterization given in Theorem 11.1, this implies
gi ∈ Q[x] . Now define the polynomials,

                                            a := `h ,
                                            b := a + dh = (` + d)h .

Then a, b ∈ Z[x] ⊆ Q[x] and a 6= b. In fact, b − a = dh, whose only irreducible monic factors in
Q[x] are x and x − 1. Moreover, for 1 ≤ i ≤ n,

               ρa,b (fi ) = (1 − fi )a + fi b = a + fi (b − a) = `h + dfi h = (dfi + `)h = gi .

Thus ρa,b (T ) ⊆ Q[x] , and hence T ,→ Q[x] via a and b satisfying the additional hypothesis.

Remark. Lemma 15.24Sholds just as well for infinite T , provided d, `, and k exist with the
requisite properties, i.e., f ∈T {|f (λ)| : λ ∈ (0, 1)} is bounded and all coefficients in elements of T
share a common denominator.                                                                            

Lemma 15.25. Let λ ∈ C \ {0, 1} and S be any finite subset of Q[λ]. Then S ,→ Qλ .

Proof. Let f1 , . . . , fn ∈ Q[x] be such that S = {f1 (λ), . . . , fn (λ)}. Set T := {f1 , . . . , fn }. Then let
a, b ∈ Z[x] be as in Lemma 15.24, and set

                              c := a(λ) ,                               d := b(λ) .

We claim first that c 6= d: If λ is transcendental over Q, then c 6= d follows immediately from the
fact that a 6= b. If λ is algebraic over Q, then let p ∈ Q[x] be the minimal (monic) polynomial of
λ. Since λ ∈ / {0, 1}, p is neither x nor x − 1, and thus p - b − a. It follows that a(λ) 6= b(λ), i.e.,
c 6= d. This proves the claim.
     Finally, we have ρa,b (T ) ⊆ Q[x] by Lemma 15.24. Evaluating both sides at λ, we then get
ρc,d (S) ⊆ Qλ by Lemma 7.3. Thus S ,→ Qλ .

Theorem 15.26. For any nontrivial sPV λ such that Qλ contains a unit of Z[λ] other than 1, and
for any S defined as in Theorem 15.12, ΣS ,→ Qλ .

Proof. By Theorem 15.12, there exists a finite set Y ⊆ ΣP ⊆ Z[λ] such that ΣS ⊆ Qλ (Y ). By
Lemma 15.25, Y ,→ Qλ . By Fact 15.23(3), Qλ (Y ) ,→ Qλ (Qλ ) = Qλ . Hence ΣS ,→ Qλ .

Using Fact 15.23(2),

Corollary 15.27. For any nontrivial sPV λ such that Qλ contains a unit of Z[λ] other than 1,
Qλ is relatively dense (in either R or C, as λ ∈ R or λ ∈ C \ R, respectively).

    Since {0, 1} ⊆ ΣP , it follows trivially that {0, 1} ,→ ΣP . Therefore Qλ ({0, 1}) = Qλ ,→ Qλ (ΣP ).
However, ΣP is closed under λ-extrapolation, i.e., Qλ (ΣP ) = ΣP . Hence Qλ ,→ ΣP . Since ΣP is
uniformly discrete, by Fact 15.23(1) we can use this as an alternative proof that Qλ is uniformly
discrete.

                                                        118
Corollary 15.28. For any sPV λ such that Qλ contains a unit of Z[λ] other than 1, Qλ is a
Delone set.

   Because Qλ ⊆ ΣP , and ΣP is a Meyer set, we have,

Corollary 15.29. For any nontrivial sPV λ such that Qλ contains a unit of Z[λ] other than 1,
Qλ is a Meyer set.

Remark. Alternatively, we note that if A ,→ B ,→ C and A, C are Meyer sets, then B is also
Meyer. In this case we have ΣS ,→ Qλ ,→ ΣP , and hence Qλ is a Meyer set.               
    The next lemma (actually its corollary) has the same conclusion as Corollary 15.29 but with
different hypotheses; it does not assume that λ is sPV.

Lemma 15.30. For any λ ∈ C, if Rλ is discrete, then Rλ − Rλ is uniformly discrete.

Proof. The lemma is trivial if λ ∈ {0, 1}, so assume otherwise. If Rλ is discrete, then Rλ is uniformly
discrete by Corollary 3.5 (and furthermore, Rλ = Qλ ). We now have

                    Rλ − Rλ = Qλ − Qλ = Qλ ({0, 1} − {0, 1}) = Qλ ({−1, 0, 1}) ,

the last equality due to Lemma 2.11. Rλ contains the three distinct values 0, λ(1 − λ), and
2λ(1 − λ), which form a 3-arithmetic progression (see Corollary 11.4), and thus it is clear that
{−1, 0, 1} ,→ {0, λ(1−λ), 2λ(1−λ)}. We therefore get Rλ −Rλ ,→ Rλ ({0, λ(1−λ), 2λ(1−λ)}) = Rλ
by Fact 15.23. It then follows from the same Fact that Rλ − Rλ is uniformly discrete.

Corollary 15.31. For F being either R or C, and for any λ ∈ F , if Rλ is discrete and relatively
dense in F , then Rλ is a Meyer set.

Proof. By Lemma 15.30 and Definition 12.8.

                                                  120
15.3.3       A Concise Proof of Theorem 12.14
In this subsection, we use affine embedding to give an alternate, shorter proof of Theorem 12.14.
The proof we give here is less informative than the original one, as it completely obscures any
connection with cut-and-project sets.

Definition 15.35. For n ≥ 1, we will say that a point set S ⊆ Rn is sub-Meyer if S −S is uniformly
discrete.

    Fact 12.11 implies that if S ⊆ Rn is sub-Meyer and relatively dense in Rn , then S is a Meyer
set. We can identify C with R2 in the usual way.
    Recall that D = C \ C = {λ ∈ C | Rλ is not convex} = {λ ∈ C | Rλ is discrete} (Definition 2.21
and Corollary 3.5).
    The next theorem is a strengthened version of Lemma 15.30, and some of the proof is cribbed
from there.

Theorem 15.36. Qλ (S) is sub-Meyer for any λ ∈ D and finite S ⊆ Q(λ).

Proof. Fix λ ∈ D and finite S ⊆ Q(λ). We have that Qλ is uniformly discrete by Corollary 3.5.
Letting T := S − S, we see that T is a finite subset of Q(λ). We have that λ is algebraic by Theo-
rem 7.11 (which is subsumed by Theorem 8.2), and thus Q(λ) = Q[λ]. It follows by Lemma 15.25
that T ,→ Qλ . Applying Qλ (·) to both sides and using Fact 15.23, we get Qλ (T ) ,→ Qλ (Qλ ) = Qλ ,
and thus Qλ (T ) is uniformly discrete (again by Fact 15.23). Finally, using Lemma 2.11, we have
Qλ (S) − Qλ (S) = Qλ (S − S) = Qλ (T ), which proves the theorem.

Theorem 15.37. Qλ (S) is sub-Meyer for any sPV λ and finite S ⊆ Q(λ).

Proof. Let λ be any sPV number. By Theorem 15.36 it suffices to show that λ ∈ D. The case where
λ ∈ R was proven by Pinch and is Proposition 12.4. The case where λ ∈ / R is by Proposition 12.5.


15.4       Higher Dimensions
For certain λ, we can use relative density results in R to prove relative density in higher dimensions.
We have already shown (Proposition 6.1, and again in Theorem 15.7) that Q1+ϕ (= R1+ϕ ) is
relatively dense, where ϕ is the golden ratio, and similarly for Q2+√2 and Q2+√3 in Theorem 15.7.
    The next two lemmas allow us in some cases to prove relative density in higher dimensions
based on relative density in R.

Lemma 15.38. For any λ ∈ R and any S ⊆ Rn (for n ≥ 1), if Qλ is relatively dense in R and
Qλ (S) contains the vertices of a parallelepiped with nonzero n-dimensional volume, then Qλ (S) is
relatively dense in Rn .

Proof. We first prove the special case where S = Cn := {0, 1}n ⊆ Rn , the corners of the unit
hypercube in Rn . In this case, we show that Qλ (Cn ) ⊇ Qλ × · · · × Qλ (n-fold Cartesian product)
by induction on n.21 Assuming Qλ is relatively dense in R, the right-hand side is clearly relatively
dense in Rn , which proves the lemma in this special case. Afterwards, we show how the general
case follows easily.
 21
      In fact, equality holds, but we will not need this.


                                                            121
    In the special case, the statement above is trivial for n = 1, where C1 = {0, 1} and hence
Qλ (C1 ) = Qλ . Now assume n ≥ 1 and that the statement above holds for n, i.e., Qλ (Cn ) ⊇ (Qλ )×n .
In Rn+1 , it is easily checked that Qλ (Cn × {b}) = Qλ (Cn ) × {b} for b ∈ {0, 1}. Whence

                           Qλ (Cn+1 ) = Qλ (Cn × {0, 1}) ⊇ Qλ (Cn ) × {0, 1} .

That is, for every v ∈ Qλ (Cn ), we have v × {0, 1} ⊆ Qλ (Cn+1 ), and from this we get

                               Qλ ({v} × {0, 1}) = {v} × Qλ ⊆ Qλ (Cn+1 ) .

This holds for all v ∈ Qλ (Cn ), and so by the inductive hypothesis, it holds for all v ∈ (Qλ )×n .
Thus,
                            (Qλ )×(n+1) = (Qλ )×n × Qλ ⊆ Qλ (Cn+1 ) ,
as we wished to show.
    Now in the general case, we can assume without loss of generality that S = {xb | b ∈ {0, 1}n } ⊆
  n
R , where the xb form the corners of a nondegenerate parallelepiped. We can index the points so
that there exists an invertible R-linear map ` : Rn → Rn that maps each b ∈ {0, 1}n to xb − x0 .
Letting S 0 := `−1 (S − x0 ), we see that S 0 = {0, 1}n , and thus Qλ (S 0 ) is relatively dense in Rn by
the special case proved above. By Lemma 12.1722 we have

                    x0 + `(Qλ (S 0 )) = x0 + Qλ (`(S 0 )) = x0 + Qλ (S − x0 ) = Qλ (S) .

The left-hand side is seen to be relatively dense in Rn because this property is preserved under
invertible R-linear maps and under translation.

Lemma 15.39. For any λ ∈ R and S ⊆ C, if Qλ is relatively dense in R and S includes three
non-colinear points, then Qλ (S) is relatively dense in C.
Proof. For any z ∈ C such that Im(z) > 0, define ∆z := {0, 1, z}. To say that S contains three
non-colinear points is equivalent to saying that ∆z ,→ S for some such z. Thus it suffices by
Fact 15.23 to show that Qλ (∆z ) is relatively dense in C for all such z.
   Fix a z := x + yi for some arbitrary x, y ∈ R with y > 0. Since Qλ is relatively dense in R, we
must have λ ∈/ [0, 1]. Let σ := λ(1 − λ). Then σ < 0, and both σ and 2σ are in Qλ :

                          σ = λ ?λ 0 ,                        2σ = λ ?λ (1 − λ) .

Consider the four points

                            a := 0 ?σ 1                            b := 1 ?σ 0
                            c := 0 ?2σ z                           d := 1 ?2σ z

All four points are in Qλ (∆z ) by Lemma 2.25(1): a and b are in Qσ ⊆ Qλ ⊆ Qλ (∆z ), and c and d
are in Q2σ (∆z ) ⊆ Qλ (∆z ). A little calculation shows that

             a=σ                                 b=1−σ
              c = 2σz = 2σx + 2σyi               d = (1 − 2σ) + 2σz = 1 − 2σ + 2σx + 2σyi

Thus a, b, c, and d form the vertices of a parallelogram in C, and this parallelogram is nondegenerate,
since σ 6= 1 − σ and 2σy 6= 0. It follows that Qλ (∆z ) is relatively dense in C by Lemma 15.38.
 22
      where R = R, M = N = Rn , and f = `


                                                    122
     Recall from Section 14.1 that for integer n ≥ 3, the point set Pn ⊆ C is the set of vertices of the
regular n-gon, located so that {0, 1} ⊆ Pn and Im(z) ≥ 0 for all z ∈ Pn (and so the unit interval
[0, 1] forms the bottom base of the n-gon).

Corollary 15.40. For any λ ∈ R, if Qλ is relatively dense in R, then Qλ (Pn ) is relatively dense
in C for all n ≥ 3.

    The following corollary covers all the sets shown in Figures 10–22, showing that they are all
relatively dense in C.

Corollary 15.41. The following sets are all relatively dense in C:
                                                       √
  1. Qλ5 (P5 ) and Qλ5 (P10 ) (recall λ5 = 1 + ϕ = (3 + 5)/2)

   2. Qλ7 (P7 ) and Qλ7 (P14 ) (cf. Equation (42))

   3. Q2+√2 (P8 )

   4. Q2+√3 (P12 )

   5. Qλ9 (P9 ) and Qλ9 (P18 ) (cf. Equation (42))

   6. Qλ15 (P15 ) and Qλ15 (P30 ) (cf. Equation (42))

Proof. Consulting the table in the proof of Proposition 14.5, we see that λn has field norm ±1 for
n ∈ {5, 7, 9, 15} and is thus a unit of Z[λn ]. Applying Corollary 15.29, we get that Qλn is relatively
dense in R for these n.23 Theorem 15.6 also implies Q2+√2 and Q2+√3 are relatively dense in R.
We apply Corollary 15.40 to all these sets.

Corollary 15.42. All the sets mentioned in Corollary 15.41 are Meyer sets.

Proof. For S be any of these sets, we have that S − S is uniformly discrete by Propositions 14.4,
14.5, and 14.9. Now we apply Fact 12.11(2) to S.

Proposition 15.43. The (1 + ϕ)-convex closure of a regular dodecahedron in R3 is relatively dense
in R3 .

Proof. Let λ := 1 + ϕ and let D be a regular dodecahedron in R3 . Choose some pair of opposite
pentagonal faces F and F 0 of D. The set of corners of each of F and F 0 is congruent to P5 , and
so Qλ (D) includes a congruent copy of Qλ (P5 ) in each of the (parallel) planes containing F and
F 0 . By the previous proposition, each copy contains the corners of a nondegenerate parallelogram
(rhombus, actually), and these rhombi can be chosen so that the one in F 0 is a translation of the
one in F . See Figure 25.
  23
    Isolated cases of these results are proved elsewhere in the paper. We have that Q1+ϕ is relatively dense in R,
either by Proposition 6.1 or by Theorem 15.6. By Theorem 15.21, Qλ7 is relatively dense in R.

15.5    Trivial sPV λ
In this subsection, we show relative density of Qλ in some cases for trivial sPV λ.

Proposition 15.44. Let λ ∈ C be a trivial strong PV number. If Qλ contains an integer other
than 0 or 1, then Qλ is relatively dense in either R or C as λ ∈ R or λ ∈ C \ R, respectively.

Proof. Suppose α is in Qλ ∩ (Z \ {0, 1}). Then Qα is relatively dense in R because it is periodic
(Theorem 10.12). Further, Qα ⊆ Qλ (Corollary 2.26). By Fact 12.3, λ is either a Z-integer or a
nonreal quadratic integer. In the former case, Qλ is clearly relatively dense in R. In the latter case,
Qλ contains three non-colinear points, and thus Qα (Qλ ) is relatively dense in C by Lemma 15.39.
Since Qα (Qλ ) ⊆ Qλ (Qλ ) = Qλ (Lemma 2.25), the result follows.

Proposition 15.45. If λ ∈ C \ R is trivial sPV and Qλ contains a point z 6= 0 such that Re(z) ∈
{0, 1/2}, then Qλ is relatively dense in C.
                                                                                                  √
Proof. By Fact 12.3 (q.v. Lemma 10.2), z is a non-real quadratic integer. If Re(z) = 0, then z = i n
for some positive n ∈ Z. But then, 0 ?z z = z 2 = −n ∈ Qz ⊆ Qλ (Corollary 2.26), and  √ hence Qλ is
relatively dense in C by Proposition 15.44. Similarly, if Re(z) = 1/2, then z = (1+ib 4n − 1)/2 for
some integers b and n with b odd and n > 0. It follows that z ?z 0 = (1 − z)z = (1 + b2 (4n − 1))/4 ∈

by Proposition 15.44. If n = 1 = ±b, then z = e±iτ /6 , whence −1 = z 3 = 0 ?z (0 ?z z) ∈ Qz ⊆ Qλ ,

                                               125
                                   Part III: Bent Paths

16      The λ-convex closure of a bent path
We continue to use ? without subscript to denote ?λ .
    This part of the paper is dedicated to proving Theorem 3.9. We sequester the proof in this
way because it uses some concepts and techniques that are not used anywhere else in the paper,
particularly, winding number and some basic homology and homotopy theory. We only need a few
facts about these:

     • For every loop ` in C (see the start of Section 3 for definitions) and every point x not on `,
       ` has a well defined winding number about x, which is an integer indicating the number of
       times ` “wraps around” x—positive for counterclockwise, negative for clockwise.

     • If two loops are homologous in C \ {x} (that is, their difference can be expressed as the sum
       of boundaries of continuous images of disks in C \ {x}), then they have the same winding
       number about x.

     • Any two loops that are homotopic in C \ {x} (that is, one can continuously deform one loop
       into the other, staying within C \ {x} and keeping the endpoint fixed) are also homologous
       in C \ {x}, and thus have the same winding number about x.

     • The winding number of a finite sum of loops—about some x not on any of the loops—is the
       sum of the winding numbers of the individual loops about x.

     • If x, y ∈ C and ` is a loop in C \ {x, y} such that there is a path from x to y that does not
       intersect `, then ` has the same winding number about y as it has about x.

Definition 16.1. A path in C is bent if it does not lie within any single straight line.

     Theorem 3.9 can then be restated as follows:

Theorem 16.2. Qλ (c) = C for any λ ∈ C \ [0, 1] and any bent path c.

Definition 16.3. Let c : [0, 1] → C be a path. A subpath of c is any path d : [0, 1] → C that starts
at some point c(a), follows c, and ends at some point c(b), where 0 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 1. That is, there
exist 0 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 1 such that d(x) = c(ρa,b (x)) for all x ∈ [0, 1].
    The loop closure of c, denoted lcl(c), is the loop obtained by first following c (double speed)
from c(0) to c(1), then along a straight line from c(1) back to c(0). It can be parameterized thus:
                                      
                                        c(2x)               if 0 ≤ x ≤ 1/2,
                          lcl(c)(x) =
                                        ρc(1),c(0) (2x − 1) if 1/2 ≤ x ≤ 1.

     The proof of Theorem 16.2 uses the following lemma:

Lemma 16.4. If c : [0, 1] → C is a bent path that does not include any nonempty open subset of
C, then c includes a subpath d : [0, 1] → C with the following properties:

  1. d lies entirely in a closed half-plane whose boundary passes through its endpoints d(0) and
     d(1), and

                                                 126
  2. there exists a point x ∈ C such that lcl(d) has nonzero winding number about x (which is not
     on lcl(d)).

Proof. Let A, B, and C be three noncolinear points along c. We can assume without loss of gen-
erality that A = c(0), B = c(1/2), and C = c(1) (otherwise, take an appropriately reparameterized
subpath of c). By our assumption about c not filling any space, we can choose a point x in the
interior of the triangle 4ABC that does not lie on c. We first show that there is a subpath e of
c that satisfies the second condition of the lemma with respect to x. Let t be the (oriented) loop
formed by tracing the perimeter of 4ABC, starting at A, going to B, then to C, then back to A.
Clearly, t has winding number ±1 about x. (It is not necessary, but we can assume that t goes
counterclockwise, so its winding number about x is +1.) Now, t is evidently homologous to the
sum of the following three loops (in fact, the difference is empty):

   • the loop closure `1 of c,

   • the loop closure `2 of the path obtained by following c backwards from B to A, and

   • the loop closure `3 of the path obtained by following c backwards from C to B.

Since the winding number around x is invariant under homology of loops in C \ {x}, and the
winding number of a sum is the sum of the winding numbers, it follows that the winding numbers
(around x) of `1 , `2 , and `3 sum to 1. Thus at least one of these three loops has nonzero winding
number around x. If it is `1 , then we take e := c; otherwise, if it is `2 , then we let e be c restricted
to [0, 1/2] (reparameterized); and otherwise (if it is `3 ) we take e to be c restricted to [1/2, 1]
(reparameterized). Then e and x satisfy the second condition of the lemma with d := e (but not
necessarily the first). See Figure 26. (Note that the orientation of e does not matter here, because
the winding number will be nonzero regardless of orientation.)


                                               B




                                                         x2
                                                                            c

                                     x3
                                                         x1

                            C                                           A



Figure 26: The curve c and the triangle 4ABC. For each i ∈ {1, 2, 3}, the loop `i has nonzero
winding number around the point xi .


   We now find a subpath d of e that satisfies both conditions. Let x be as above (so that lcl(e)
has nonzero winding number about x), let L be the line through P := e(0) and Q := e(1), and let
L0 be the line through x parallel to L. The situation might look like Figure 27.


                                                   127
                                                                                       e


                                                    x                                             L0




                                  Q     Z       W                                P                 L




Figure 27: The curve e and the point x. (The small loop that follows the curve e from Z to W
then straight back to Z has nonzero winding number around x.)


      Let H be the open halfplane of C with boundary L and containing x. Then by continuity,
e−1 (H) is an open subset of (0, 1), and hence is the disjoint union of at most countably many open
intervals I0 , I1 , I2 , . . . ⊆ (0, 1). For i = 0, 1, 2, . . . , let ei be the subpath of e restricted to Ii . Now we
claim that there can be only finitely many i such that ei intersects L0 . Indeed, suppose there were
infinitely many such i, say i0 , i1 , i2 , . . . . For all j ∈ {0, 1, 2 . . .} let aj be the left boundary of Iij ,
and pick some zj ∈ Iij such that e(zj ) ∈ L0 . By the continuity of e, we must have e(aj ) ∈ L. The
sequence z0 , z1 , z2 , . . . has some accumulation point z ∈ [0, 1]. Take some monotone subsequence
zj0 , zj1 , zj2 , . . . converging to z, where j0 < j1 < j2 < · · · . If this sequence is increasing, then we
have zjk < ajk+1 < zjk+1 for all k, and if it is decreasing, then we have zjk+1 < ajk < zjk for all
k. In either case, the sequence aj0 , aj1 , aj2 , . . . also converges to z, but then since aj ∈ e−1 (L) and
zj ∈ e−1 (L0 ) for all j, and both e−1 (L) and e−1 (L0 ) are closed, we have z ∈ e−1 (L) ∩ e−1 (L0 ) = ∅.
Contradiction. This establishes the claim.
      Now by the above claim, we have ei1 , . . . , ein intersect L0 for some natural number n and some
indices i1 , . . . , in , and no other ei intersect L0 . Set ` := lcl(e). For 1 ≤ j ≤ n, let rj < sj be
the boundary points of Iij , and let `j be the loop closure of the subpath eij of e that goes from
e(rj ) to e(sj ) (note that e(rj ) and e(sj ) both lie on L). We now can express ` as the finite sum
`1 + `2 + · · · + `n + p, where p := ` − (`1 + `2 + · · · + `n ). This decomposition (for the curve shown
in Figure 27) is illustrated in Figure 28.
      The winding number of ` about x is nonzero, because e satisfies the second condition of the
lemma with respect to x. This winding number is the sum of the winding numbers (about x) of
`1 , . . . , `n , and p. There is no contribution from p to the winding number, because p stays entirely
in the open halfplane bounded by L0 and containing L (thus it cannot wrap around x). It follows
that there must be some `j that has a nonzero winding number around x (e.g., the small loop
through Z and W in Figure 27). Since eij lies entirely in H (except for its endpoints), we can take
d := eij , which then satisfies both conditions of the lemma.

   We first prove the special case of Theorem 16.2 where λ is real. This is Proposition 16.5, below.
Afterwards, we will explain how to modify the proof for nonreal λ.

Proposition 16.5. Qλ (c) = C for any λ ∈ R \ [0, 1] and any bent path c.

Proof. We can assume that λ > 1 by Fact 2.13. If c includes a nonempty open subset of C, then
we are done by Proposition 3.8, and so from now on we assume that this is not the case. Then


                                                        128
                                             x                                         L0
